Editorial 
Opportunities from the bond sell-off  
Strategy summary: The global bond sell-off is creating tactical 
and medium-term opportunities. In the US, September’s yield 
surge has improved the risk -reward in 10 -year government 
bonds, presenting an attractive entry point for investors with a 
6-12-month horizon. We believe bond and money markets are 
overly hawkish about the Fed outlook, given disinflation is likely 
to return as impact of oil prices and tariffs ease next year. 
In equities, we stick to quality, especially sectors riding the AI -
led earnings boom. The upcoming Q3 earnings season is likely 
to show AI -investments are powering sectors beyond the 
technology and communications services, such as financials, 
materials and power and electrification. We remain constructive 
on the semiconductor and memory segments. We also expect 
the US Dollar to peak in the next few weeks along with short -
to-medium-term US bond yields. This creates opportunities for 
AUD corporate bonds, Emerging Market USD bonds and gold. 
Attractive risk -reward in US 10 -year government bonds.  
The US 10-year bond yield rose more than 50bps in September 
(biggest monthly rise since 2022) and 115bps this year, amid 
expectations of economic resilience, higher Fed rates, fuel -
driven inflation, AI-related capital demand and fiscal concerns. 
Valuations appear close to fair value, improving the 6-12-month 
risk-reward. Bond arithmetic suggests losses of less than 1% if 
the yield rises to 6%, vs. more than 10% gains if it falls to 4.5%. 
Resilient US economic activity, but softer -than-expected 
inflation. September data showed robust US employment 
(jobless claims, ADP) and core capital-goods orders. However, 
consumer confidence remains depressed, real disposable 
income is flat and households are drawing on savings to sustain 
spending. Against this divided backdrop, core PCE inflation - 
the Fed’s preferred gauge - was softer than expected at 0.2% 
m/m and 3.0% y/y. A revised method to measure PCE inflation 
also indicate that annual core PCE inflation was 35bps lower 
than previously reported, narrowing the gap with CPI inflation. 
Markets appear overly hawkish on the Fed. Money markets 
price about 85bps of tightening over the next 12 months. A 
strong labour market (September’s non -farm payrolls data is 
due tonight) may justify one or two further hikes, but aggressive 
tightening after the sharp rise in bond yields – which has 
already tightened financial conditions - could threaten financial 
stability. US small- and mid-cap equities have already fallen 8–
9% from recent peaks over the past couple of months, 
materially underperforming large caps, as energy and 
borrowing-cost concerns intensify. W hile some Fed speakers 
remain hawkish, we  agree with New York Fed President 
Williams’ that there is no urgency to tighten. We see little 
chance of an October Fed rate hike and expect inflation data 
for Sep-Nov determining whether the Fed moves in December. 
Stay with quality equities and AI beneficiaries.  US large 
caps have absorbed the rise in bond yields relatively well, 
supported by expectations of robust earnings growth led by AI-
related investment. We continue to favour quality companies 
and sectors benefiting from this AI cycle. Semiconductors and 
memory remain preferred exposures: structural AI demand 
should extend the memory upcycle, while long -term supply 
agreements provide better revenue visibility. The opportunity is 
also broadening beyond technology and communication 
services into financials, materials, power and electrification. 
Opportunities in AUD corporate bonds and gold.  The USD 
rebound alongside higher US yields offers a tactical opportunity 
to add higher -yielding Emerging Market USD and non -USD 
bonds. We remain positive on AUD corporate bonds, which 
offer a yield premium over comparable US credit. Our 
constructive medium-term AUD/USD view could add returns for 
USD-based investors over the next 6 –12 months. We would 
also use gold’s recent pullback to add exposure, supported by 
sustained Emerging Market central bank demand. We continue 
to view USD 4,000/oz as important technical support for gold.  
—  Rajat Bhattacharya

Top client questions 
  Do you expect more RBA rate hikes after its less hawkish 
guidance? What is your view on the AUD and AUD bonds? 
Our view: We expect the RBA to leave rates unchanged, but 
retain the option to hike again should inflation intensify. We 
maintain our medium-term constructive AUD view and maintain 
our opportunistically bullish AUD corporate bonds idea. 
Rationale: The RBA’s forward guidance remains data dependent, 
explicitly allowing further tightening if needed as inflation remains too 
high.  At 4.60%, Australia’s cash rate is currently 60–85bp above the 
Fed’s 3.75–4.00% range. Thus, even with our two additional 25bp s 
Fed hikes expectation, Australian rates would hold a modest positive 
rate differential. We expect the AUD to strengthen once the Fed 
signals that US rates are near their peak, which should allow resilient 
Australian fundamentals to reassert themselves. Key risks are 
persistently high US yields, weaker China growth or a sharper 
deterioration in global risk sentiment. 
We believe risk-reward has become more attractive for AUD bonds 
following the bear -flattening of the AUD yield curve. Markets are 
already pricing in one more RBA hike through end-2027, presenting 
an attractive opportunity to earn a yield even as increasingly 
restrictive financial conditions reinforc e disinflation over time. 
Meanwhile, the positive interest rate differential with the US supports 
the currency outlook, supporting the case for AUD bonds. 
—  Vincent Tan, Senior Investment Strategist 
— Cedric Lam, Senior Investment Strategist 
— Iris Yuen, Investment Strategist 
  What does the incoming US data tell us about the path for 
US 10-year bond yields from here? 
Our view: The risk -reward in US 10 -year yields has turned 
attractive following their move higher amid incoming US data. 
Rationale: Recent US data has indeed started to show softer-than-
expected inflation. However, market technicals are likely temporarily 
delaying a move lower in bond yields, with reports suggesting the  
rise in yields is creating forced selling amongst hedge funds and real 
estate investment trusts. Nevertheless, we do not expect this to be 
an extended selloff. We have initiated an opportunistic bullish idea 
on US 10-year government bonds this week and continue to believe 
this idea offers an attractive risk/reward on a 6-12-month horizon as 
yields become attractive enough to entice buyer flow to return. On a 
broader allocation basis, we retain our structural preference for 3-7-
year maturities in bonds. 
— Cedric Lam, Senior Investment Strategist

We remain positive on AUD corporate bonds amid 
Australia-US interest rate differentials and positive 
outlook for the AUD

### Page 5

Standard Chartered Bank 
WS Global CIO Office | 2 October 2026

Top client questions (cont’d) 
  How should investors position in the GBP ahead of the UK 
budget announcement next month? 
Our view: We maintain a medium-term constructive GBP view, 
but keep near-term exposure measured ahead of the 28 October 
Budget. We would view any pre -Budget weakness as an 
opportunity to gradually build GBP exposure. 
Rationale: The government’s decision to adjust the pension triple 
lock from 2030 is an early indication of greater willingness to tackle 
longer-term spending pressures, reinforcing Chancellor Healey’s 
commitment to fiscal discipline and meeting the fiscal rules. Markets 
are likely to scrutinise the Budget closely for evidence that this 
discipline extends to near -term tax and spending decisions. A 
credible Budget should help contain pressure on UK government 
bond yields and support GBP. Any fiscal slippage remains a key risk.  
Near term, we prefer measured GBP exposure as elevated US yields 
and repricing towards further Fed tightening could continue to 
support the USD. Over the medium term, we expect GBP to rebound 
as the Fed eventually signals the peak of its tightening cycle. 
—  Vincent Tan, Senior Investment Strategist 
—  Iris Yuen, Investment Strategist 
  How would different US mid-term election outcome 
scenarios affect the outlook for US equities? 
Our view: We believe election outcomes will have a limited 
impact over the next 12 months. We remain positive on US 
equities driven by fundamental earnings growth.  
Rationale: Over the past 50 years, the ruling US President’s party 
has retained control of both chambers of Congress on only two 
occasions (1978 and 2002). All other elections result ed in an 
opposition sweep or a divided government.  
Based on the latest prediction markets, it appears that an opposition 
sweep (both the House and Senate flips from Republican to 
Democrats) is more likely than a divided government ( in which the 
House flips to Democrats , while the Senate remains under 
Republican control). A scenario where Republicans retain a majority 
is least likely per prediction markets. 
While mid -term elections may introduce near -term volatility, we 
caution against overreaction. Historically, the average return for the 
S&P 500 following an opposition sweep is 17% over the next 12 
months, but the average return following a divided government is not 
significantly worse, at 15%. We prefer to base our investment views 
on fundamental earnings growth, where investor attention should 
focus on the upcoming Q3 earnings season. 
—  Jason Wong, Senior Equity Analyst

Standard Chartered Bank

Top client questions (cont’d) 
  What is the outlook for  the memory chip and broader 
semiconductors industry? 
Our view: We remain positive on semiconductors and memory 
chips. Structural AI demand should prolong the memory 
upcycle while long-term agreements improve revenue visibility. 
Rationale: Recent industry commentary points to tighter memory 
supply-demand conditions through 2028, reinforcing our view that AI 
demand is likely to extend the memory upcycle. Furthermore, adoption 
of long-term agreements improves revenue and earnings visibility. 
Despite debates around “Pacing the Frontier”, we have seen a flurry 
of new model releases, along with the introduction of new consumer 
AI agents capable of comparing prices online and making 
purchases. These are early days, but the path is open for a business-
to-agent (B2A) economy where businesses increasingly interact with 
AI assistants acting on customers’ behalf. As these applications are 
increasingly inference intensive, wider adoption should support 
demand for memory, with its share of global semiconductor 
revenues projected to rise from 30.7% in 2025 to 66.7% in 2027. 
Against this backdrop, we maintain our Opportunistic idea on Global 
Semiconductors. Given memory  chips’ cyclical nature, investors 
should avoid overconcentration in memory names and maintain a 
diversified exposure across Big Tech and semiconductors. 
—  Ryan Goh, Investment Strategist 
  Does a US bond yield stress test raise the risk to your 
constructive view on US equities? 
Our view: We have a target for the S&P 500 index of 8400 over 
the next 12 months. A scenario of a sustained rise in the US 10-
year government bond yield to 5.6% could lead to negative 
returns from the current S&P500 level (7,666). 
Rationale: Our base case 12-month target for the S&P 500 is 8,400, 
which assumes 2027 S&P 500 EPS of USD 420 and a 20x forward 
P/E multiple. A rise in bond yields would have an impact on equity 
valuations, as a higher discount rate reduces the present value of 
future earnings. W e stress -test our target under different 10 -year 
yield and earnings scenarios.  
A rise in bond yields would arguably be associated with a stronger 
growth environment and hence, even higher earnings. However, 
assuming unchanged EPS at our base case of USD 420, a 
(sustained) rise in the 10 -year bond yield to 5.6% would lead to 
negative returns from the current level  if not offset by a further 
improvement in earnings growth . However, in o ur base case  we 
expect the 10-year yield is more likely to fall from here. 
—  Fook Hien Yap, Senior Investment Strategist

### Page 3

Report Introduction
Our proprietary dbDataInsights survey shows cyber threats are evolving faster than organisations can adapt
Source: dbDataInsights survey of cybersecurity experts, September 2026; Cybersecurity Survey 2026, Deutsche Bank Research.
“It’s the only thing that can shut down my business.”
That was the punchline from a conversation I had recently with the CEO of a large
privately held financial services company. This was his comment when I followed up

as to why cybersecurity risk is the number one concern that “keeps him up at night”.
To help understand this risk, our dbDataInsights team surveyed more than 50
cybersecurity experts. The uncomfortable conclusion is that many firms are
unprepared for cyber threats. Cyber risk is increasing as AI adoption accelerates.
—89% agree the threat landscape is evolving faster than organisations can adapt.
—78% say the gap between highly and poorly prepared organisations is widening.

What has changed with AI advancement? Cyberattacks have become faster,
cheaper, more deceptive and more scalable. Capabilities once reserved for
sophisticated actors are now broadly accessible to a new generation of profit-driven
criminals. At the same time, rising geopolitical tension has made cyber operations
an increasingly attractive low-cost, deniable tool for nation states. Corporates and
investors therefore need to understand their broader exposure and critical

infrastructure risk in their supply chain and with stakeholders.
Among other autonomous incidents, the recent Hugging Face incident
highlighted AI’s cyberattack potential. AI agents caused harm without
direction from a human attacker, with weak guardrails and no human
judgement to restrain them. Beyond the systemic questions it raised, the attack
highlights three additional concerns: (i) AI operates at a speed and scale humans
cannot match, with agents taking over 17,000+ actions in four days; (ii) the intrusion

began in the data-processing pipeline, where AI platforms are uniquely exposed;
and (iii) the agents escaped their test environment through a widely used third-party
tool representing supplier risk in an unfamiliar form.

### Page 4

Report summary and notable takeaways
Secular trends and AI adoption have widened the attack surface; the severity of losses is compounding
Source: FBI Internet Crime Complaint Center (IC3), company security advisories, OECD, company reports, Deutsche Bank Research.
Secular trends have widened the attack surface. This includes rapid
digitalisation, widespread Internet of Things (IoT) endpoints, hybrid workforces,
cloud adoption and deeper supply chain dependencies. The scale of the threat

increase is reflected in data from the FBI Internet Crime Complaint Center (IC3),
which shows cybercrime complaints more than doubled from 2019 pre-pandemic
baseline of ~467,000 to ~1.0mn in 2025, with a 3x expansion relative to 2017 levels.
Barriers to entry are lower; attack tools are now sold “off the shelf”. Today,
cybercrime marketplaces, available on the dark web, look less like the underworld
portrayed in films and more like an online software store. Buyers can access AI-

powered tools that scan for software vulnerabilities (see top-right chart showing
reported CVEs after the release of cyber capable frontier models), write malicious
code at speed, and provide access to malware that rewrites its own code to avoid
detection as it runs. Ransomware-as-a-service (RaaS) takes it further, renting out
ready-made attack kits, often in exchange for a portion of the profits.
Impersonation is becoming harder to detect. Hackers can now generate more

realistic phishing messages, while voice cloning and deepfakes make impersonation
convincing enough to gain access unnoticed. With stolen credentials, attackers often
aren’t hacking in, they are simply logging in.
The rise of loose cybercriminal collectives. Some of the most damaging recent
attacks have been linked to loosely organised, often English-speaking networks such
as ShinyHunters and Scattered Spider. The latter has been widely named as the

group behind the 2025 Marks & Spencer cyberattack, in which the hacking group
deployed RaaS. AI is enabling these groups to become larger and faster, widening
the gap between the speed of attack and a defender’s response.
The severity of losses is compounding. A 2026 IBM study puts the average breach
at a record USD ~5.0mn, up 12% YoY, but the impact on large companies can run
into hundreds of millions or even billions. Estimates for global cyber insurance

premiums (per Munich Re) point to a rise from roughly USD 7.0bn in 2020 to USD
15.0bn in 2025, with expectations to reach USD 28.0bn by 2030.

### Page 5

5
Among the more concerning survey takeaways is that a large-scale and
significant systemic incident is now the base case.
— 83% expect a significant systemic attack within two years and 81% expect it to be
moderately, very, or extremely severe for economic activity and markets.
— 81% say AI favours attackers today, with not one respondent putting defenders
ahead of attackers. Only 20% expect defenders to be ahead in five years.
Geopolitical tensions coupled with rising cyber capabilities have transformed

cyberspace into a digital battlefield between global powers. Intent has moved
towards espionage and disruption of not only critical infrastructure (e.g., healthcare,
finance, power, industrial systems, defence) but private companies as well. Chinese
President Xi Jinping has linked cybersecurity to national security and economic and
social stability, with China’s public statements similarly emphasising the protection
of critical infrastructure. At the same time, the US’s June 2025 Executive Order on

Cybersecurity reflects its continued focus on national cyber resilience.
Europe relies on US cyber tools while its start-ups lag in funding. Reliance on US
security vendors exposes European firms to potential access, update or export
restrictions over the long-term. Shrinking this gap will prove difficult given US cyber
start-ups attract nearly 6x the venture and growth funding of European peers. And
while the timing of quantum computing (which could break widely used encryption

methods) remains uncertain, it could further deepen Europe’s dependency.
Ultimately, cyber preparedness is a financial variable. Investors should ask what
firms spend on security versus AI adoption, how well they know their vulnerabilities,
and how fast they can recover.
— Only 24% of respondents think organisations have sufficient visibility into the
threats relevant to them, even though 57% saw cybersecurity budgets rise over
the past 12 months.

— 81% agree that cybersecurity related laws and regulation cannot keep pace, and
65% doubt leadership understands cyber risk well enough.

### Page 6

6
When cyber weakness = share price consequence…
Investors are more aware of cyber risk today, but they rarely sell a stock due to
perceived cyber vulnerabilities (although we are aware of some who have).
—What is the potential impact? Our dbDataInsights event study of 47 breaches
shows a cumulative breach-attributed return of c. -2.9% within the first two
trading days and c. -6.0% through first 20 days trading days. The erosion then
continues more gradually, reaching c. -6.7% nine months after disclosure (see

slide 13).
Which industries are most vulnerable?
—Our survey respondents point to (1) government & public sector (52%), (2)
financial services (50%), (3) energy and utilities (46%) and (4) healthcare and life
sciences follow (39%, rising to 48% among US respondents).
We should caveat, that high sector exposure does not mean every company in it is
equally vulnerable. Some firms in these “exposed” sectors may very well be better
prepared given they have built specialist teams and defence protocols for decades.

What about access to credit and ratings? Credit and rating providers are paying
attention. Moody’s and S&P both cut their outlook on Jaguar Land Rover within two
months of its 2025 hack. Today, this type of impact is not widespread. But if risk
escalates through a systemic incident or an AI-driven step change in attacks these
mechanisms, including ESG ratings, could reprice asset ownership or impact access
to credit. In fact, some lenders are already pricing it. Journal of Corporate Finance

research using US syndicated loan data found borrowers with persistently higher cyber
risk pay 4–13bp more.
The bottom line? While a broader systemic attack is hard to prepare for, corporates
and investors should be asking questions about vulnerabilities, security investment,
preparedness and recovery should an attack occur. This is especially true for
industries quickly adopting AI capabilities and / or a high threat landscape.

dbDataInsights Cybersecurity Expert Survey methodology
—This survey was conducted among 54 cybersecurity professionals across the US
and Europe in September of 2026. Questions took the form of either multiple

choice selections or free form responses. The dbDataInsights team received the
responses anonymously, but respondents were required to work or consult in
cyber security roles
—Who took the survey? On self-identified roles, around 80% sit inside an
organisation’s cybersecurity function, with the remainder working independently
(15%) or in academia and research (6%). The most common titles were security
architect (23%), Chief Information Security Officer (16%) and security analyst (9%),

alongside Head of Security, Deputy CISO, Incident Response Lead and Security
Operations Lead.
—Only a small minority of respondents work for cyber resellers, thus survey views
are primarily attributable to those working within an organization on
cybersecurity.
—Survey participants were also asked for their perspectives on cyber resilience and
governance, what is misunderstood, alongside emerging threats, risk to AI
adoption and the influence of geopolitics.

—While responses were anonymous, in some cases we conducted follow up
interviews with certain participants where contact details were made available.
Source: dbDataInsights survey of cybersecurity experts, September 2026; Cybersecurity Survey 2026, Deutsche Bank Research.

Risk from AI-enabled attacks and added AI adoption vulnerabilities
Insider threats and supply chain compromises remain a growing concern
“The fear of AI writing undetectable malware is … overblown. Attackers were
never short of malware, since they can buy it ready made along with initial
access. Good detection looks at behaviour rather than exact code, so a rewritten
payload still has to do the same suspicious things.”
“Frontier AI models are currently the main hype. Automated phishing and

scams are underappreciated, yet they are responsible for the bigger
monetary losses at the moment.”
“There are too many organisations relying on the same providers for
critical services and this means single points of failure.”
“A corporation might be very secure but if they rely on a third party that
does not have the same level of security posture, [it] could be
compromised via this third-party (basically their weak link).”
“Existing employee login behaviour and usage of AI is underappreciated –

it’s what poses the biggest threat to a breach.”
“No company can guarantee they won’t be breached, so there needs to
be more focus put on the ability to recover and survive an attack.”
Source: dbDataInsights survey of cybersecurity experts, September 2026; Cybersecurity Survey 2026, Deutsche Bank Research .

### Page 11

11
Experts believe governments are not keeping pace, and no one owns AI oversight
How experts surveyed by dbDataInsights view governance and regulation
—AI adoption is outpacing internal controls. As one expert put it: “Companies
are pushing the use of AI more to their employees without the proper
mechanisms and policies in place to prevent misuse or attacks initiated by the AI
systems themselves.”
—Regulation is struggling to keep pace. 81% of respondents agree that

cybersecurity-related laws and regulations are failing to keep up with
technological change, and 54% say regulatory requirements can improve security
but may also slow innovation. One respondent highlighted “the levels of
uncertainty, the long running nature of the problems, and the rapidity of change.”
—Responsibility for AI oversight remains unsettled. International bodies are the
leading choice, supported by 50% of respondents. No option commands a

majority, underscoring the lack of consensus on who should oversee AI
development.
—Better board questions may not guarantee better decisions. The survey
points to more informed questioning by corporate leadership, yet 65% doubt that
leadership teams understand cyber risk well enough to make informed business
trade-offs. Measuring activity more effectively than outcomes further complicates
oversight.
—Accountability is becoming personal. Only 43% say accountability for cyber risk

is clearly defined in their governance framework, yet the consequences now reach
the top as a handful of CEOs have left or gone into early retirement after
financially consequential cyber breaches.
Who should primarily oversee AI development?

Material cyber breaches can cause a notable drop in share price
Our dbDataInsights study found a 2.9% drop on average within two trading days, with the cumulative breach-attributed return of -6.0% on trading day 20.

Source: dbDataInsights, Bloomberg Finance LP., Deutsche Bank Research, Company filings. Event study of reported cybersecurity incidents, 2015 -2025, 34 US and 13 European companies across nine sectors.
What typically happens to a company’s shares when a breach is reported?
—Short-term impact: The market prices the breach immediately. Our model shows
cumulative breach-attributed (i.e. isolating only the part of the share price move
we can pin on the breach itself) returns are c. -2.9% within two trading days of

at one year, although the one-year point estimate (our single best-guess figure)
should be treated with caution given the width of the confidence band.
—Pre-breach price behaviour: We find no evidence of information leakage ahead
of disclosure. While the breach component for the [-10,-1] window (ten trading
days before the news broke) is +0.04%, the corresponding t-stat value of 0.03 falls
notably below the usual bar for significance (c.2). As such, we conclude the market

had not begun reacting before the announcement.
Event study methodology
—Sample: 47 publicly reported cybersecurity incidents across the US and Europe
over 2015-2025, measured against each company’s regional sector benchmark.
Requirement of 5 years of stock returns pre-attack and 1 year of post-attack.
—Day 0: the day an incident was first publicly reported or disclosed.
—Baseline: We use five years of pre-incident share-price performance to
estimate: (i) Beta (β): how sensitive a company’s returns are to movements in its

sector and (ii) Alpha (α): the extent to which the company typically outperforms
or underperforms its sector after adjusting for that sensitivity.
—Attribution: post-incident returns are split into sector-relative returns, alpha
(α) and breach (abnormal returns attributed to the disclosure).
—95% confidence interval: Range within which we can say with 95% confidence
that the real share price impact lies, based on our sample data.
Total return attribution

95% Confidence Interval Breach impact negative at 95% confidence Cumulative impact of cyberincident

Marks & Spencer (Reported Apr 2025)
What happened?
A breach via an attack on a connected 3rd
party forced the suspension of all online
orders and led to empty store shelves.
What did it cost?
~£136mn of direct costs related to the
attack, estimated group operating profit
impact of around £300mn for the FY. M&S
also said they had received £100mn via
cyber insurance.
Coupang (Reported Nov 2025)
What happened?
A data breach exposed the records of
more than 30mn users with a former

employee reportedly behind the attack.
What did it cost?
The firm was fined more than ~625bn won
($409mn) (2/3rd directly attributable to
data leak), and also announced a $1.2bn
voucher compensation scheme.
UnitedHealth (Reported Feb 2024)
What happened?
Hackers reportedly used stolen credentials
to access a subsidiary’s (Change
Healthcare) systems, stealing data and
deploying ransomware.
What did it cost?
Total costs exceeded $3bn, with the CEO
summoned to testify before Congress,

where he confirmed that a $22mn ransom
was paid.

What happened?
A data breach saw hackers expose
sensitive customer information, including
145.5mn Social Security numbers and
more than 200k credit card numbers.
Attackers reportedly moved inside Equifax
systems for 76 days before being detected.
What did it cost?
Settlements, fines and legal costs
exceeded $1bn, while the firm also spent
more than $1.5bn on tech upgrades

following the incident.

Notable cyber attacks: how they happened, estimated costs and share price reaction, cont.
Nike (Reported Jan 2026)
What happened?
A cybercrime group leaked what it claimed
was 1.4TB of internal Nike data (appearing
to cover metrics such as product
specifications and pricing), following expiry
of a ransom deadline.
What did it cost?
While Nike did confirm it was investigating
the incident, it did not report any
operational disruption. Underperformance

shown in the chart does not appear to be
related to the breach.
T-Mobile (Reported Aug 2021)
What happened?
An attacker reportedly targeted an
exposed router as an entry point, before
using brute force attacks to gain further
access to T-Mobile systems.
What did it cost?
In 2022 the firm agreed to pay $500mn to
settle a class-action lawsuit ($350mn
payment to class members  plus $150mn
on security spend) but has since suffered
further data breaches, contributing to an

additional $31.5mn regulatory settlement.
Coinbase (Reported May 2025)
What happened?
Attackers bribed at least one support
agent at a connected 3rd party to access
Coinbase support systems and steal
customer data, then tricked users into
sending crypto.
What did it cost?
Coinbase refused to pay the $20mn
ransom demanded by the attackers to
keep the attack quiet. Instead they
reimbursed affected users and cited costs
of up to $400mn. The stock impact was

short-lived, though MSCI applied an ESG
score deduction.
Jaguar Land Rover (Reported Sep 2025)
What happened?
Production was shut down for five weeks
due to what was deemed a ransomware
attack; though no ransom was asked for
(later reporting suggested potential Russia
links were being investigated).
What did it cost?
JLR posted £485mn Q2’ FY26 loss (vs.
£398mn profit in Q2’ FY25), with an
additional £196mn in “cyber-related costs”.
These impacts filtered through to the

share price of JLR’s Parent (Tata Motors),
while UK economic impacts were
estimated at £1.9bn.

### Page 16

Targets go beyond tech and can also impact critical infrastructure
MGM Resorts (Reported Sep 2023)
What happened?
A vishing call to the IT help desk was reportedly used to reset credentials, after which
ransomware took down hotel systems, bookings and casino floor operations for several
days.
What did it cost?
MGM declined to pay a ransom. Around $100mn hit to third-quarter 2023 results plus
roughly ~$10mn of additional one-time expenses related to the incident.

Caesars Entertainment (Reported Sep 2023)
What happened?
The same social-engineering group as in the MGM incident targeted an outsourced IT
support vendor, gaining access to the loyalty programme database and member data.
What did it cost?
Caesars paid roughly $15mn of a reported $30mn demand and kept customer-facing
operations running.
NHS / Synnovis (Reported Jun 2024)
What happened?
Ransomware against pathology provider Synnovis significantly reduced its capacity to

process tests leading to delays / cancellations of over 11,000 appointments.
What did it cost?
Estimated 2024 direct operational financial impact of around GBP 32mn for Synnovis,
several times the profit reported in the prior year, plus wider NHS disruption costs.
Colonial Pipeline (Reported May 2021)
What happened?
A compromised VPN credential without multi-factor authentication allowed attackers to
access the network where they subsequently deployed ransomware. The operator shut the

pipeline down, triggering fuel shortages (c. 70% of gas stations in some states ran out of
fuel) across the US East Coast.
What did it cost?
A $4.4mn ransom was paid, of which about $2.3mn was later recovered by the US
authorities.
Bitget (Reported Sep 2026)
What happened?
Attackers exploited a vulnerability in a 3rd party security product to steal internal network
credentials, forge withdrawal instructions, bypass risk controls and ultimately steal crypto.

What did it cost?
An estimated $388mn worth of funds was transferred out by the attackers. While the firm
reimbursed affected users via its User Protection Fund, there were significant outflows
following the incident as users moved to take funds off the platform.
Progress Software (Reported May 2023)
What happened?
Attackers exploited a zero-day SQL injection vulnerability in Progress Software’s MOVEit file
transfer system. This was then used by attackers to steal data from firms running the

Debbie Jones | dbDataInsights | Under Attack | October 2026

### Page 17

Cyber breaches can impact consumer behaviour
Roughly 4 in 10 consumers we surveyed say they would stop buying from a company they shop with after a known breach or attack.
Source: dbDataInsights monthly consumer survey, Deutsche Bank Research. EU Average includes France, Germany, Italy and Spain.
How will customers respond to a data breach once it occurs?
dbDataInsights survey of consumer responses, % of respondents
How will customers react once the breach is resolved?

Consumer responses among those who would either stop buying or were unsure, % of respondents
Consumer surveys suggest that cyber breaches can undermine customer
loyalty.  Results of our dbDataInsights Consumer survey show the potential
mindset of a consumer after a cyber breach occurs:
—Across the US, UK and EU average, 39–41% say they would stop buying, while only
27–28% would continue. Around a third are unsure.
—Among those who would stop buying or are unsure, only 18–22% say they would

return as normal. Another 30–35% would buy selected products only, 17–20%
would not return, and 28–29% remain unsure. Note that this implies a range of
13-15% believe they would not shop at the store again after the breach is
resolved.
Admittedly, these stated intentions may overstate actual switching, particularly for
essential purchases where alternatives are limited. Nevertheless, the findings
suggest that cyber breaches that put customer privacy at risk can influence

purchasing decisions, especially if they have alternatives.

dbDataInsights Consumer Survey Methodology
—Our proprietary survey conducted in 2026 (January – September) asked

consumers how they would respond if a company they shopped at regularly
was involved in a data breach or cyberattack, and how they would respond
once the disruption was resolved. This monthly survey covers households and
consumers in Germany, France, Italy, Spain, the United Kingdom, and the
United States.
—For each country referenced above, a nationally representative sample of more
than 500 households is surveyed each month (over 600 households in the US).

Collectively, this meant over the course of the survey (January – September
2026), we surveyed almost 30,000 households.

### Page 18

18
Material cyber incidents have contributed to negative credit outlooks
The infrastructure exists for cyberattacks to have a greater impact on credit quality should cyber disruption continue to accelerate
Source: S&P, Moody’s, Fitch, Deutsche Bank Research.
—Credit rating agencies consider both pre-breach resilience frameworks and
post-breach financial impacts. Agencies assess pre-breach cyber preparedness
through a lens of mitigating risk, while post-breach credit impacts centre on

financial capacity to withstand the resulting shock (considers scale and length of
the disruption); highly leveraged issuers with limited liquidity are seen as more
vulnerable. Negative outlook changes at JLR and Equifax illustrate the potential
credit impact. Fitch generally considers permanent reductions in cash generation
more credit-significant than temporary cash-flow delays.
—Ratings agencies are moving towards continuous monitoring. In 2021,

Moody’s invested $250mn in BitSight, which produces daily security ratings.
Moody’s has since built BitSight’s ratings into its credit tools, including Credit
Catalyst and EDF-X, for uses such as underwriting and ongoing monitoring of
borrowers and suppliers. This means lenders can see a company’s exposed
vulnerabilities as a potential indicator of corporate cyber risk.
S&P Global:
“The increasing frequency of cyberattacks and the potential for rapid deterioration in credit

profiles after an attack are risk factors relevant for our rating assessments.”
Moody’s:
“Cyberattacks can undermine the creditworthiness of companies, organizations and
governments, across all sectors and geographies, hitting revenues, increasing costs, and eroding
customer trust.”
Fitch Ratings:
“Although cyber events cannot be predicted with great accuracy, the associated risk can be
managed through proactive strategies, technology, policies, and vigilance.”

“The chief executive officer and board of directors hold ultimate responsibility for managing
cyber risk.”
Example rating actions where cyber was a named factor
Outlook changes due to operational disruption, financial cost and reporting delays

outlook change  in 2019. This was the first time
it had done so and followed the 2017 attack on
Equifax cited on slide 14 .

Source: MSCI ESG Ratings Methodology, Morningstar Sustainalytics ESG Risk Ratings, SASB Materiality Map, Deutsche Bank Resear ch.

MSCI and Morningstar Sustainalytics both assess data privacy and security through management of the

and a breach can cut it directly – more severe controversies draw larger deductions that persist for
longer.

### Page 20

IV. Investment, risk and opportunity

### Page 21

21
Europe’s dependency on US tech is firmly in the spotlight
Access to frontier AI models is now a security question; the funding gap for Europe will be hard to close
Source: Tikehau Capital – Tikehau Capital Barometer of European Investment in Cybersecurity – 7th edition 2026, Bloomberg Finance LP, Deutsche Bank Research.
—Growing cyber risk creates increased dependency on US cyber tech. Growing
concern over the cyber capabilities of frontier AI models has made ensuring

access to such technology a national security priority. US dominance over the AI
tech chain (e.g. chips, model development, etc), its willingness to impose AI-
related controls / restrictions, and previous pressure against Europe over tech
regulations has amplified concerns that dependence on US tech represents a
strategic threat to European sovereignty.
—Funding is the structural challenge. US cybersecurity start-ups attract almost 6x
the funding of European peers, and US-domiciled firms dominate the major

indices – 89% of the Nasdaq CTA Cybersecurity index and 93% of Global X
Cybersecurity ETF (which seeks to track the performance of Indxx Cybersecurity
Index) by weight. Europe has also seen home-grown firms such as Darktrace
acquired by US companies, though this reflects broader market trends rather
than anything cyber-specific. Ultimately, the US has a greater exit strategy for
start-ups as well.
—Boosting homegrown solutions a focus, but also a risk. EU policymakers are

seeking to address the dependency; e.g. the EU’s Cybersecurity and AI Action Plan
includes “scaling up Europe’s AI capabilities for cybersecurity” as one of its three
objectives. However, there are also risks associated with this endeavour; as one
dbDataInsights survey participant put it, “for most European organisations, the
bigger near-term danger would be moving too fast to less capable providers.”
—The wider relationship adds to the exposure. A more transactional

international environment, alongside Europe’s dependency on the US for energy
supplies and defence support, compounds the risk. Recent US-EU diesel
negotiations serve as a pertinent example of this, whereby the US reportedly
considered a diesel export ban to compel a release of European diesel reserves.

### Page 22

22
Cybersecurity as an opportunity
Tailwinds include demand related to keeping operations secure with AI acceleration and consolidation tailwinds
Source: dbDataInsights, Deutsche Bank Research. Investors, defined as those who invest in stocks/ shares (either individually or through a broker/advisor) on a regular basis , were asked whether they perceived cybersecurity / data privacy as a risk or an opportunity for investors in 2026.
Broadly, DB’s company research view is that cybersecurity becomes even

more important in the AI era. Whereas cybersecurity has historically been viewed
as a blocker/drag on innovation and operational speed, it is now viewed as a
strategic enabler for secure AI adoption.
In particular, network security may see renewed growth after being viewed as a
more mature category. Rising AI usage should drive additional compute and data-
centre capacity, expanding the infrastructure requiring segmentation and
protection. Agentic workloads may also generate lower-throughput but more

frequent and persistent interactions across models, tools and data sources,
increasing the volume and complexity of network-level inspection.
Vendor consolidation has been a structural trend in cyber for some time, though not
necessarily linear. That said, the current environment may accelerate the
consolidation tailwinds as customers look to close security gaps that may arise from
a fragmented tool stack.
See further commentary from DB’s company research team here.

Our March 2026 “personal investor” survey showed a more positive view
among “ESG” investors. 58% in the US and 52% in the UK saw cybersecurity/data
privacy as an opportunity. Among all investors, US respondents favoured
opportunity over risk (46% vs 30%). UK views were more balanced (38% vs 40%).

### Page 24

Appendix A - Attack types
Top Attack types What it is How AI is helping attackers
Malware Software built to damage, disrupt or gain unauthorised access AI writes harmful software faster. Google has found malware that rewrites its own code while running to avoid detection, and criminal marketplaces selling AI malware-building tools.
Phishing Deceptive messages that trick people into giving up sensitive information Where AI’s effect is clearest: fluent, personalised lures without the old spelling and phrasing giveaways. Google says state-backed groups use AI to mass-produce them, and AI can fake voices and video.

Denial of service (DoS) Flooding a system so it cannot serve legitimate users Little reported evidence so far. AI can help find weak points and automate traffic floods, but the attack itself stays fairly simple.
Distributed denial of service
(DDoS)
The same attack launched from many
compromised devices at once
AI can accelerate the development and automation of attack tooling, while large-scale
botnets provide the distributed capacity behind hyper-volumetric DDoS attacks.

Man-in-the-middle (MitM) Intercepting or altering traffic between two parties Mainly indirect. AI-written phishing pages and deepfake voices make it easier to trick people into connecting through an attacker or approving fake sign-ins.
SQL injection Malicious SQL queries injected to manipulate backend database operations AI tools scan sites and spot flaws in database code faster, so less-skilled attackers can find weaknesses that once needed real expertise.

Cross-site scripting (XSS) Scripts injected into web pages to hijack user sessions As with SQL injection: AI speeds up finding vulnerable pages and writing the malicious code.
Zero-day exploits Attacking a software flaw before a patch is released A major new development. In May 2026 Google reported the first zero-day it believes was developed with AI. Its September 2026 report says the bigger effect is attackers misusing known flaws faster, before fixes land.

Advanced persistent threats
(APTs)
An intruder staying hidden in a network for long-
term
Google reports state-backed groups using AI across the whole attack, from target
research and lures to tooling and sifting stolen data.
Ransomware Encrypting data or locking device and demanding payment to release it NCSC expects AI to add to the global ransomware threat by lowering the skill needed to break in. AI also helps criminals find the most extortable data.

IoT exploitation Abusing weakly secured connected devices for entry AI can scan the internet for unpatched cameras, routers and storage devices and identify which flaws they carry.
Debbie Jones | dbDataInsights | Under Attack | October 2026
Source: Deutsche Bank Research, National Cyber Security Centre, Google Threat Intelligence Group, Cloudflare, FBI Internet Crime Complaint Center (IC3)

Appendix B - European vs US cyber regulation
Europe US Federal
Debbie Jones | dbDataInsights | Under Attack | October 2026
Regulation Key requirements
NIS2 Directive In force 2023, transposition deadline Oct-24. Covers 18
sectors (up from seven) and creates an EU vulnerability
database. In-scope firms must manage cyber and
supply-chain risk and notify authorities of significant
incidents. Management bodies must approve and
oversee cyber risk measures, with potential liability for

infringements.
Cybersecurity Act In force 2019. Establishes an EU-wide certification
framework for ICT products, services, processes and
managed security services, and made the EU
cybersecurity body ENISA permanent.
Cyber Resilience Act In force 2024, main obligations from 2027.
Manufacturers and retailers of products with digital
elements must deliver security by design, a defined
support period and security fixes within it, plus
mandatory vulnerability management and disclosure.

Digital Operational
Resilience Act (DORA)
In force 2023, applies from 2025. Financial-services
specific, covering own-firm and third-party risk. Requires
an ICT business continuity policy, regular resilience
testing and disclosure of major incidents, and gives
regulators oversight of critical ICT providers.
Cyber Solidarity Act In force 2025. Creates a European Cybersecurity Alert
System to detect threats and share cross-border
warnings, plus a Cyber Emergency Mechanism to boost

preparedness and response.
Regulation Key requirements
White House Accord on
Super Intelligence
(2026)
Emphasises self-regulation to ensure AI safety, with a call for
external evaluations and the possibility of formal regulation in
future. Not legally binding; signed by President Trump and
leading AI companies.
Executive Order on
Cybersecurity (2025)
Highlights risk from foreign actors and orders further action
on defending digital infrastructure, securing the most vital

and governance.
CIRCIA (passed 2022,
not yet in effect)
In-scope entities, focused on critical infrastructure, must
report covered incidents to CISA within 72 hours and
ransomware payments within 24 hours of payment.
Gramm-Leach-Bliley Act
(amended 2024)
Financial institutions must explain information-sharing
practices and safeguard customer data, running a security
programme covering risk assessment, service-provider
oversight and incident response.

Federal Information
Security Modernization
Act (2014)
Applies to federal agencies and contractors: system security
plans, continuous monitoring, incident response capability
and mandatory incident reporting.
Federal Acquisition
Regulation (cyber
provisions 2016)
Governs federal procurement, setting baseline cyber
requirements for suppliers so cyber risk is built into
government contracting. Agencies may impose stricter
provisions.

## Document 464: US Equity Views_ AI capex, required revenues, and what's priced in the AI trade

Source PDF: all report pdf/US Equity Views_ AI capex, required revenues, and what's priced in the AI trade.pdf
Report source: Goldman Sachs Publishing
Date: 2026-09-24
Pages: 16
Extraction status: ok

### Page 2

An update on AI and US equities

Both stocks and investor views relating to the AI trade have been exceptionally 
volatile during the last few months. Most of the volatility has been concentrated in the 
AI infrastructure complex, but the latest earnings season also drove sharp moves in the 
hyperscalers and among software stocks. 
Below we address three of the most common AI-related questions we have received 
from clients in recent weeks: 
1. What is the outlook for AI capex? 
2. What revenues are required to generate a sufficient return on recent AI 
investments? 
3. What outlook for AI is the equity market pricing?

1. What is the outlook for AI capex? 
The largest US hyperscalers are on track to spend $800 billion in capex in 2026 and 
consensus estimates imply they will spend $1.1 trillion in 2027. Consensus 2027 
capex estimates have been revised higher by $175 billion since the start of the Q2 
earnings season. Commentary from the hyperscalers during earnings season described a 
supply-constrained environment and large revenue backlogs, and these factors are 
driving the companies to tap both debt and equity markets to support additional 
investment spending. Based on consensus estimates, capex in 2027 is on track to reach a

### Page 3

larger share of GDP than any technological investment cycle since the railroad build-out 
in the late 1800’s. 
Our baseline forecast is that hyperscaler capex spending will increase substantially 
in 2027 but that the rate of both capex growth and upside surprises to consensus 
estimates will diminish relative to recent quarters. GS equity analysts estimate that 
hyperscaler capex will register $1.2 trillion in 2027 and $1.4 trillion in 2028. Their 
estimates are $130 billion and $100 billion above the consensus estimates, respectively, 
but nonetheless imply a deceleration in capex growth from nearly 100% in 2026 to 54% 
in 2027 and 12% in 2028.

The need for external financing and potential supply bottlenecks will likely 
constrain the rate of capex growth next year. Hyperscaler capex spending now 
exceeds cash flow from operations, which means large further growth will increasingly 
need funding via debt and equity issuance. While hyperscalers have added leverage, 
their balance sheets do not appear to be a binding constraint and their interest coverage 
ratios remain extremely healthy. Our credit strategists instead point to concentration in 
the investment grade market as the more relevant bottleneck, which will require 
additional funding from private credit and equity issuance. Potential government 
restrictions on data center construction or an intentional slowdown in frontier model 
roll-out could also act as potential constraints on spending, as could shortages of labor, 
power, memory, or other inputs. Constraints on capex represent a risk to the near-term 
earnings of AI infrastructure beneficiaries, but could help ease investor concerns about 
overbuilding.

### Page 4

Going forward, token demand growth will need to outpace declining token prices to 
support continued growth in investment spending. Frontier models are currently a 
key source of demand for hyperscaler compute. However, the rise of competitive 
open-source models has contributed to a decline in average token prices. Measures of 
frontier token demand slowed in July, simultaneous with sell-offs of many AI 
infrastructure stocks, but accelerated again in August. 
For most AI infrastructure companies, decelerating AI investment spending 
suggests decelerating EPS growth. AI infrastructure companies, and semis in 
particular, have been the clearest beneficiaries of the capex surge. Continued upward 
revisions to consensus 2027 capex estimates should support continued positive 
revisions to consensus 2027 EPS estimates for many AI infrastructure stocks, but the 
magnitude of revisions will likely diminish relative to what occurred in H1 2026.

### Page 5

2. What revenues are required to generate a return on AI investments? 
Our equity analysts estimate that the hyperscalers would need to generate annual 
AI revenues of roughly $300 billion in the next few years to break even on their 
average annual capex investments in 2026 and 2027. These estimates reflect their 
expectations for average annual AI capex in 2026 and 2027 for the US hyperscalers, 
including AMZN, GOOGL, META, MSFT, ORCL, and SPCX. The breakeven revenues 
required for this capex represent the sum of annual depreciation and operating 
expenses. Exhibit 8 provides an estimated sensitivity of required AI revenues the 
hyperscalers must generate in different return on invested capital (“ROIC”) scenarios. 
These required revenue estimates are anchored on average annual capex in 2026 and 
2027 and would argue for even larger estimates based on cumulative capex.

The hyperscalers’ current estimated AI revenues remain below the amount that 
would allow them to break even on their capex, but those revenues are growing 
quickly and revenue backlogs are sizable. The hyperscalers have started to monetize 
their AI investments. Year/year cloud revenue growth for AMZN, GOOGL, MSFT, and 
ORCL has accelerated from 25% in 2024 to 48% in Q2 2026. In dollar terms, annualized 
cloud revenues in excess of their 2024 growth rate registered nearly $70 billion in Q2 
and have been roughly doubling during the past few quarters. The three major US public 
cloud companies (AMZN, GOOGL, MSFT) have also announced a combined revenue 
backlog of $1.7 trillion, which provides line of sight into these companies’ ability to meet 
required revenue targets.

Hyperscalers include AMZN, GOOGL, META, MSFT, ORCL, and SPCX. “Required”

### Page 7

The magnitude of capex in 2026 and 2027 points to even larger “required” annual 
revenues in the next few years for the “application layer” of companies buying 
much of that compute from the hyperscalers. These revenues reflect the additional 
costs and profit margins of application companies on top of the “required” hyperscaler 
revenues selling compute. EBIT margins for large-cap software companies have 
averaged 40% during the past five years. We utilize EBIT margins rather than gross 
margins to include the costs of both training and inference, and assume additional 
expenses alongside compute. The range of potential required revenues is wide 
depending on which companies constitute the application layer and how their costs are 
structured.  
Exhibit 11 provides a range of potential “required” revenues based on hyperscaler 
ROIC and application layer EBIT margins. For example, the application layer would 
have to generate over $1 trillion in annual revenues if those companies had a 30% EBIT 
margin and the hyperscalers generated a 10%-20% return on invested capital. 
The “required” application layer revenues that would justify current capex are large 
but potentially achievable. For example, estimates of global advertising and software 
spending each equal roughly $1.5 trillion in 2026. Our economists estimate that total 
annual AI US labor productivity gains that will potentially accrue to capital total roughly 
$1.8 trillion. However, not all of these gains will necessarily accrue to the infrastructure 
and application layer companies. In addition, another year of strong capex growth in 
2028 would raise the hurdle further. AI-related revenues and capex needs would be 
substantially larger accounting for physical AI or the potential emergence of AGI.

### Page 8

3. What outlook for AI is the equity market pricing? 
While market participants express skepticism about the durability of AI 
infrastructure stocks’ earnings growth, that view is at least partially priced. The 
median AI infrastructure stock has de-rated from 32x consensus forward earnings in 
April 2026 to 22x today. In addition to tracking standard P/E multiples, comparing 
recent changes in market cap to recent changes in earnings can signal how long the 
market expects current earnings strength to persist. Among various AI infrastructure 
baskets, that ratio remains most elevated among optical networking, electrical grid, and 
liquid cooling stocks while expectations appear most conservative among 
semiconductors and utilities. 
Semiconductor valuations appear to embed particularly skeptical views regarding 
the sustainability of current earnings strength. Valuations have declined sharply 
among Semis stocks, which is typical among cyclical companies with strong near-term 
growth but uncertain medium-term growth. We believe the investment opportunity is 
more compelling in fabless chip stocks than in memory stocks. Our equity analysts are 
Buy-rated on NVDA and AVGO but Neutral-rated on MU.

### Page 9

Memory stock prices imply earnings will decline in coming years, but the degree of 
pessimism does not appear particularly extreme relative to past cycles. Memory 
stocks currently trade at a 2-year forward P/E of 4x, half their 15-year average, but 
Memory firms currently enjoy gross margins of roughly 80%, more than double their 
historical average. To bring the current P/E of memory stocks to its 15-year average, 
earnings in coming years would need to decline by roughly 50%. While this decline is 
large in absolute terms, memory is a cyclical industry and has experienced larger EPS 
declines, including outright negative earnings, during previous cycles. This pricing will 
prove too pessimistic if 2028 forecasts are realized, but many investors have expressed 
skepticism about rotating back into higher beta pockets of the infrastructure trade as 
capex growth begins to normalize.

### Page 10

In contrast, the fabless chip stocks also trade at discounted valuations but have had 
less extreme margin expansion. Margins for the largest fabless chip stocks are only 
modestly above their historical average. Earnings in the next few years would need to 
decline by about 10% to bring the P/E back to its historical average. This is similar to the 
magnitude of historical realized cyclical earnings declines for the group.

The likelihood of decelerating capex growth should be a positive catalyst for the 
hyperscalers. We previously laid out three conditions for the hyperscalers to regain 
leadership. These include decelerating macroeconomic growth, demonstrated 
monetization of AI investment, and decelerating capex growth. Our economists believe 
US economic growth is no longer accelerating. The hyperscalers have already started to 
demonstrate signs of monetization, and we expect capex growth will slow in 2027. 
Uncertainty about the length, magnitude, and return on investment for this capex cycle 
will likely persist. However, the hyperscalers have de-rated to their lowest P/E multiples 
in over a decade, and the group now trades just four turns above the median S&P 500 
company, its smallest premium on record. While investors have recently been focused 
more on the hyperscalers’ negative free cash flows than on their earnings, visibility into 
improving FCF as revenues accelerate and capex decelerates should be supportive for 
the stocks.

### Page 11

We expect the progression of the AI trade towards application roll-out will remain a 
source of return dispersion. We believe the “winners” and “losers” from the application 
layer will take several quarters to play out, as AI applications get built and adoption 
broadens. 
The threat of disruption had weighed on the share prices of many Software stocks 
earlier this year, but the group has rebounded sharply in recent weeks. Reported 
Software fundamentals have shown little sign of AI disruption, with annual recurring 
revenue growth for the median stock in GS analyst coverage accelerating slightly in 
recent quarters. A group of Software stocks has rallied by 45% and is now within 8% of 
its high reached in late 2025. Software stocks focused on IT infrastructure and 
cybersecurity have posted the strongest returns, while enterprise management software 
remain below their previous highs.

### Page 12

Consumer-facing businesses with high switching costs have also recently emerged 
as a potential area of disruption. The increasing use of agents, including by new 
consumer-facing tools such as Muse, may disrupt the business models of some 
consumer firms. Our colleagues in Global Banking & Markets developed a basket of 
“consumer inertia” stocks (GSXUSWCH) where user stickiness is driven more by effort 
required to take action rather than product differentiation. The basket includes 
insurance, telecom, travel, subscriptions, marketplaces, and fintech. The basket has 
lagged a sector-neutral hedge by 5 pp since mid-September.

### Page 13

Enterprise AI adoption remains in early stages but spending has recently 
accelerated, suggesting the impact of AI on corporate earnings should grow 
increasingly visible in coming quarters. The Census Bureau survey suggests that 40% 
of large US firms are using AI in the production of goods and services. Our analysis of Q2 
earnings commentary suggests roughly 50% of S&P 500 companies are using AI in the 
context of productivity, but very few companies have quantified the productivity uplift 
today. However, the revenue run rates of model providers and alternative measures of 
company spending on AI have accelerated during the last several months alongside the 
increasing use of agentic harnesses. 
In contrast with past periods of market exuberance, the equity market today does 
not appear to be pricing expectations of widespread productivity gains. Baskets of 
AI productivity beneficiaries have traded roughly sideways on a relative basis YTD, 
matching the trajectory of near-term forward EPS estimates. Similarly, the measure of 
market-implied long-term growth expectations in Exhibit 22 is only slightly above the 
historical average (for details, see US Equity Views: The next phases of the AI trade).

Abbott 已察觉到这⼀裂痕，近期命令监管机构采取措施防⽌数据中⼼推⾼居⺠电费，并承诺推动
⽴法取消数⼗亿美元税收减免，限制数据中⼼在 " 农村社区 " 的建设。
但 Hinojosa 将 Abbott 的表态斥为 " 没有任何意义的双关语 " ，认为其缺乏紧迫性和可信度。
据中⼼。

大型銀行 3Q26獲利成長延續 ，但幅度收斂 ，主要
是非利息收入表現開始降溫，多數銀行認為投行案
源充足，需求只是遞延而非消失，債權相關業績因
利率走高而下滑，但股權相關業績仍有收獲；相比
之下，3Q獲利貢獻焦點一致轉向了淨利息收入 ，
業績增長得益於貸款與存款穩定成長，而財富管理
業務也提供了顯著的成長。部份銀行非利息費用成
長幅度大於收入成長幅度，導致效率比率變差。儘
管信貸品質仍健康無虞，但仍有部份銀行對潛在風
險展現謹慎態度，因而提高壞帳準備金。

展望後市，因淨利息收入的前景相對清晰，故可見
美大型銀行因此維持或上調 2026全年淨利息收入
預期，或上修 2026年股東權益報酬率 ，並重申或
下修壞帳率。相比之下，資本市場業務則需觀望，

## Document 574: 中国製ハイブリッド車の輸入制限に踏み切るEU

Source PDF: all report pdf/中国製ハイブリッド車の輸入制限に踏み切るEU.pdf
Report source: MURC
Date: 2026-10-20
Pages: 4
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

2026 年 10 月 20 日

MURC Focus

中国製ハイブリッド車の輸入制限に踏み切る EU

～日本にとっても“対岸の火事”ではない～

調査部 主任研究員 土田 陽介

1. 12 月にセーフガードを発動 
欧州連合（EU）は 10 月 12 日、輸入が急増している中国製のハイブリッド車（HV）とプラグインハイブリッド車
（PHV）を対象に、12 月よりセーフガード（緊急輸入制限）を発動すると発表した。期間は4 年間で、1 年目は HV
と PHV の年間輸入台数を計 35 万台とし、その後の増加幅は年間 6%までに制限する。EU 籍のメーカーが中
国で生産する車も対象となるが、中国メーカーの EU 域内での生産車は対象外となる。

EU は 2024 年 10 月 30 日より、中国製 EV（電気自動車）に対し、基本関税 10%に加えて、最大で 35.3%の
追加関税を課している。廉価な中国製 EV の輸入を抑制する目的からだが、一方で HV と PHV には追加関税
を課してこなかった。その結果、中国製 HV と PHV の輸入が 2025 年から急増し、EU 製の HV と PHV は劣勢
に追いやられた（図表 1）。また電動車の対中貿易収支も、HV と PHV を中心に赤字が膨らんだ（図表 2）。 
図表 1. 中国製電動車の輸入台数の推移 図表 2. 電動車の対中貿易収支

（注）EV:電気自動車、PHV:プラグインハイブリッド車、HV:ハイブリッド車 
（出所）欧州連合統計局（ユーロスタット）

（注）EV:電気自動車、PHV:プラグインハイブリッド車、HV:ハイブリッド車 
（出所）ユーロスタット

（億ユーロ）
〇EU は10月12日、輸入が急増している中国製の HV と PHV を対象に、12月よりセーフガード（緊急輸入制
限）を発動すると発表した。 
〇2025年に再登板した米国のドナルド・トランプ大統領による貿易・関税政策を保護主義だと批判する EU だ
が、その EU もまた保護主義の性格を強めている。 
〇今回の中国製 HV と PHV に対するセーフガードは、日本にとって“対岸の火事”ではない。政府と日系メーカ
ーが一丸となり、EU に対して互恵主義に基づく平等な競争条件の順守を訴えていくことが望まれる。

### Page 2

EU の 2025 年の中国製 HV と PHV の輸入台数の実績値は計 30 万台であったが、2026 年の輸入台数は
80 万台に上ると予測されている（1～7 月期の実績値は約 38 万台）。通商担当であるマロシュ・シェフチョビッチ
欧州委員は今回の措置で、2027 年の中国製 HV と PHV の輸入台数（計 35 万台）は 2026 年から半減すると
アピールしている。

とはいえ EU は、中国製 HV と PHV の輸入台数が年間 6%まで増加することを容認しているため、2027 年の
輸入台数が計 35 万台に抑制されても、セーフガード最終年の 2030 年の輸入台数は計 42 万台まで容認するこ
とになる。要するに EU は、中国との全面的な対立を回避するために、EU 市場に対する一定のマーケットアクセ
スを容認していることになる。

2. 特に中国に対して保護主義傾向を強める EU 
このように、2025 年に再登板した米国のドナルド・トランプ大統領による貿易・関税政策を保護主義だと批判す
る EU もまた、保護主義の性格を強めているが、背景には国際競争力の低下がある。一例が価格競争力の指標
である実質実効為替レートで、人民元と日本円とで比較すると、ユーロの増価が鮮明である（図表 3）。これは欧
州中銀（ECB）による高金利政策のみならず、EU の高インフレに伴う内外価格差の拡大に起因する。

高インフレが生じた理由は、そのエネルギー政策（ロシア産の安価なエネルギーの排除とそれに伴う再生可能
エネルギーへのシフト）に加えて、賃金政策（最低賃金の引上げなどに起因する物価・賃金スパイラル）の影響 に
よるところが大きい。それ以外にも、EU の執行部局である欧州委員会が環境面を中心に、いわゆる“過剰規制”
を課したことが産業競争力の低下を招いたと指摘される。

図表 3. 実質実効為替レートの比較 図表 4. EU の貿易収支

（出所）国際決済銀行（BIS）

（注）4 四半期後方移動平均値。

### Page 3

競争力を失った EU の貿易収支は安定した黒字を稼ぐことができなくなり、直近 2026 年 4～6 月期は▲220
億ユーロと、イラン発のエネルギーショックに伴うエネルギー価格高騰のあおりもあって 2023 年 1～3 月期以来
の赤字になった（図表 4）。特に EU が抱える対中貿易赤字は巨額であり、EU はその削減を重視している。今回
の中国製 HV と PHV に対するセーフガードの発動も、こうした動きの延長線上にある。

EU は雇用確保の観点から、中国企業に対して、輸出を増やすのではなくEU 域内で生産をするように求めて
いる。そのための直接投資（FDI）の受け入れに EU は積極的だが、同時に EU は防衛やインフラなど戦略産業
に関しては中国による投資や中国製品 を排除する意向を鮮明にしている。 いずれにせよ、米国に比べれば穏や
かとはいえ、EU もまた、とりわけ中国を念頭に保護主義の動きを強めている。

3. 日本も EU の保護主義対象に 
今回の EU の措置は、あくまで中国製 HV と PHV に対するものである。そのため、他国製 HV と PHV には
適用されない。日系自動車メーカーもまた日本から、あるいはトルコの生産拠点などからEU 向けに自動車（主に
HV）を輸出しているが、これらの HV と PHV は今回のセーフガードの対象にならない（図表 5）。ただし、結局の
ところ、EU の保護主義は先行き、日本にも及んでくることが懸念される。

実際、欧州委員会が 2026 年 3 月に提案した産業加速化法（IAA）に基づき、EU が公共調達や公的支援で
域内製品の優遇を図る際には、日本や韓国、トルコなどの友好国から輸入した自動車などはその対象から排除さ
れると報道されている。また日系を含めた非 EU 籍のメーカーが EU 域内で生産した自動車も、EU 域内のメー
カーからの部品調達がなされない限り、同様に IAA の対象から排除される方向にある。

収支

### Page 4

日本と EU は 2019 年 2 月 1 日に経済連携協定（日 EU・EPA）を発効しており、互恵主義に基づく自由で公
正な貿易と投資を尊重し合う関係にある。そして日本も、輸入を中心に対 EU 貿易を拡大してきた（図表 6）。しか
しながら、このように自由主義に基づく通商関係を確認した日本に対しても、EU は結局のところ、保護主義の性
格を強めつつある。

こうした傾向は 、ウルズラ・フォン・デア・ライエン欧州委員長を中心とする現体制が続く限りは修正が望みにく
い。このように整理していくと、今回の中国製 HV と PHV に対するセーフガードは、日本にとって “対岸の火事”
ではない。EU が日本に対して不当競争を強いることがないよう、政府と日系メーカーが一丸となり、EU に対して
互恵主義に基づく平等な競争条件の順守を訴えていくことが望まれるところである。

3Q26 營運成果及 4Q26 展望不如預期 
1H26因 OEM提前備貨，NB因調漲售價導致需求不佳，造成下游庫存未能
消化，新機部分零組件的備貨需求驟減，3Q26 美元營收為 130.8 百萬美元，
低於 3Q26營收財測 139~153百萬美元的下緣，調整後稅後 EPS為 7.49元，
低於原預估的 10.07元約 26.6%。4Q26下游仍在消化相關零組件，旺季效
益並不明顯，公司財測為營收 95~105 百萬美元、季減 19.7~27.4%，毛利
率 41~45%，營業費用 33.0~36.0百萬美元。將 4Q26營收預估下修 33.2%
至 32.6億元，季減 22.1%、年減 16.2%，稅後 EPS預估則下修至 3.47元。 
ASIC 專案維持在 2H27 開始貢獻營收 
ASIC專案進展順利，將有多顆 IC涵蓋高、低階應用，目前 N6製程產品已
完成設計定案，譜瑞主要提供 PCIe 5的自研 IP技術，可提供低功耗、以及
過去舊款晶片組無法支援的功能，ASIC維持在 2027年底~2028年初開始貢
獻營收，潛在市場產值約 1 億美元。伺服器相關業務公司仍專注在 PCIe 6
的銅線 ACC，並積極布局 800G及 1.6T的市場，現階段正在送樣至 CSP業
者認證中，對營運的貢獻預期仍有待 2027年發酵。 
PC/NB 旺季效益落空、且相關 IC 進入庫存調整期 
2026年主要產品應用領域 PC/NB出貨下滑，新 ASIC專案則因採用先進製
程、開發期較長，需待 2H27貢獻營收。2H26 PC/NB 上游零組件的旺季效
益落空，目前仍在消化 1H26提前備貨造成的庫存，預期最快需待 2Q27才
可恢復拉貨，預估 2026/2027年稅後 EPS為 25.39元/28.65元，分別下修
28.7%/36.1%，營收來源仍集中在消費性的 PC/NB，短期營運成長動能疲弱，
但考量 ASIC、ACC Cable 等新專案將於 2H27 發酵，評價以 2H27~1H28
獲利 37.81 元計算，維持持有評等，目標價由 710 元調降至 600 元(16X 
2H27~1H28F EPS)。 
元大觀點 
⧫  
2H26 PC/NB 零組件旺季效益落空，3Q26 營運成果及 4Q26 展望皆不
如預期，4Q26 營收將季減超過 20%。 
⧫  
N6 製程 ASIC 完成設計定案，2027 年底~2028 年初開始貢獻，潛在產
值約 1 億美元；ACC Cable 尚在驗證，目標 2027 年開始貢獻營收。 
⧫  
營收來源以 PC/NB 為主，2H26 下游品牌開始調整庫存，成長動能疲
弱，維持持有評等，目標價調整至 600 元以反映獲利預估下修。

營運分析
3Q26 NB 需求下滑，營收及獲利皆不如預期
3Q26整合 Tcon、Source Driver、Touch的產品 TTED出貨仍佳，但 1H26因 OEM提前備貨，而
NB 因記憶體漲價而調漲售價導致需求不佳，造成下游庫存未能消化，導致新機部分零組件的備貨
需求驟減，3Q26 營收 41.9 億元，季減 1.5%、年減 4.4%，美元營收為 130.8 百萬美元，低於
3Q26營收財測 139~153百萬美元的下緣，而因產品組合較差，以及售價尚未能反映成本上升，毛
利率 40.3%在財測區間 40~44%的下緣，較原預估的 41%為低，調整後稅後 EPS為 7.49元，低於
原預估的 10.07元約 26.6%。

4Q26 需求未見起色，展望不如預期
2026年 PC/NB出貨維持年減約 10%，一般型或消費類 NB需求疲弱，但公司仍看好 Nvidia RTX
Spark晶片對 AI PC 需求的刺激，品牌出貨將偏向高階機種，譜瑞 4Q26財測為營收 95~105百萬
美元、季減 19.7~27.4%，毛利率 41~45%，營業費用 33.0~36.0百萬美元。整體 NB需求不佳、
且下游仍在消化相關零組件，旺季效益並不明顯，將 4Q26 營收預估下修 33.2%至 32.6 億元，季
減 22.1%、年減 16.2%，因產品組合偏向高階，且調整產品售價的效益將逐漸展現，預估毛利率回
升至 42.2%，稅後 EPS預估則下修至 3.47元。

### Page 4

請參閱末頁之免責宣言。 台灣：IC設計 2026年 10月 29日 Parade Technologies 第4 頁，共 14頁  
產業概況  
預估 2026 年 PC/NB 出貨量年減 10%以上 
AI算力基礎建設的需求快速增加，導致記憶體報價自 2H25以來暴漲，品牌廠不僅採購成本大幅增
加，市場上的記憶體供給也被利潤較高的 AI 應用所排擠，消費性應用即使出高價也可能拿不到貨，
加上中東局勢升溫、通膨疑慮再起， PC/NB 規格未明顯提升、但售價上漲也使得整體消費力道疲
弱，2025年 PC/NB出貨經歷過關稅的衝擊，2026年需求將持續受到壓抑，預估全球 PC/NB出貨
量將年減 10~15%。 
圖 3：2026 年 PC 出貨量預估 圖 4：2026 年 NB 出貨量預估

資料來源：IDC、元大投顧預估 資料來源：IDC、元大投顧預估 
PCIe 持續升級，伺服器為驅動 Retimer 需求的主要應用 
高解析面板需求提升與影音需求提升，對於訊號的傳輸速率、頻寬、穩定性持續增加，亦驅動
PCIe/USB/DP等主流傳輸規格升級(圖 5)，同時隨傳輸速度增加、資料訊號在 PCB、連接線訊號失
真問題愈趨明顯，因此主要透過 1) 更高速 PCB板材；2) Redriver或 Retimer來解決此問題。近年
AI 興起，對資料傳輸的需求快速爆發，開發資源也轉向 AI 應用，持續推升 PCIe 規格，但消費性
產品新規格推出後需要時間滲透普及，導致較偏向消費性應用的資料傳輸規格 (如 DisplayPort、
Thunderbolt、HDMI等)升級速度開始放緩。 
圖 5：各規格傳輸速度持續提升

依據 Intel/AMD伺服器平台發展路線圖(圖 6)，Eagle Stream/Genoa 平台即開始納入 PCIe 5 規格，
Retimer亦成為主機板之標配，此外加上 1) AI伺服器對於 GPU Retimer需求上升(一般伺服器單台
2顆、對比 AI伺服器單台超過 20顆)；2) PCIe 4 & 5 企業級 SSD滲透率上升(假設每 4個 SSD需
要一顆 Retimer)；3) 伺服器自 PCIe 4 世代起逐步搭載網卡 (NIC)、且後續將逐步內建。預估
2025~2034年全球 Retimer產值年複合成長率為 13.5%，其中超過 50%貢獻將來自於伺服器應用。

### Page 10

請參閱末頁之免責宣言。 台灣：IC設計 2026年 10月 29日 Parade Technologies 第10 頁，共 14頁

公司簡介  
譜瑞科技成立於 2005年，公司主要專注於高速訊號傳輸介面與顯示晶片之研發、設計與銷售。公司產品主要分成四大類：
1) Display Port (DP) ，包含 DP/eDP TCON，因公司為 VESA 協會創始會員，對於 DP 技術方向掌握優於同業，為目前
eDP TCON 的龍頭廠商；2) High Speed Interface ，包含各種不同介面(USB、DP、Thunder Bolt、PCIe等)訊號的轉換、
增強、收發等晶片，如 retimer、redriver、converter、MUX/DeMUX、switch 等，應用在不同的終端產品； 3) Source 
Driver，主要為 SIPI source driver；4) 觸控相關產品。營收比重(3Q26)分別為：DisplayPort (DP) <30%、High Speed (PS) 
45%、Source Driver (TC) 10%、TrueTouch (TT) >15%。主要客戶為 APPLE、華為、三星、OPPO、各大 NB品牌廠商與
伺服器 OEM廠商。競爭對手為 IDT、TI、聯詠、Altera Lab、Microchip等公司。

### Page 11

請參閱末頁之免責宣言。 台灣：IC設計 2026年 10月 29日 Parade Technologies 第11 頁，共 14頁

ESG 分析  
分別藉由「在 ESG議題上的曝險」和「個別公司在 ESG議題上的執行力」兩個面向來衡量公司於 ESG上的表現。 
► ESG 總分： 譜瑞-KY整體的 ESG風險評級屬於低等風險，於 Sustainalytics資料庫所覆蓋的公司中排名亦排行於略微
領先的位置，但在半導體設計及製造行業的公司中排名略微領先同業。 
► 在 ESG 議題上的曝險： 譜瑞-KY的整體曝險屬於低等水準，略優於半導體設計及製造行業的平均水準。公司較需要改
善的 ESG 議題包含人力資本、資源利用、商業道德等。 
► 個別公司在 ESG 議題上的執行力：譜瑞-KY在針對重大 ESG議題的管理水準和執行力屬低等。公司對於投資者及公眾
披露資訊能力弱，在關鍵領域缺乏政策和計劃，不過目前該公司並未涉及任何與  ESG 相關的重大爭議。

投資評等說明 
買進：根據本中心對該檔個股投資期間絕對或相對報酬率之預測，我們對該股持正面觀點。此一觀點係基於本中心對該股之發展前景、財務表現、利多題
材、評價資訊以及風險概況之分析。建議投資人於投資部位中增持該股。 
持有-超越同業：本中心認為根據目前股價，該檔個股基本面吸引力高於同業。此一觀點係基於本中心對該股發展前景、財務表現、利多題材、評價資訊
以及風險概況之分析。 
持有-落後同業：本中心認為根據目前股價，該檔個股基本面吸引力低於同業。此一觀點係基於本中心對該股發展前景、財務表現、利多題材、評價資訊
以及風險概況之分析。 
賣出：根據本中心對該檔個股投資期間絕對或相對報酬率之預測，我們對該股持負面觀點。此一觀點係基於本中心對該股之發展前景、財務表現、利多題
材、評價資訊以及風險概況之分析。建議投資人於投資部位中減持該股。 
評估中：本中心之預估、評等、目標價尚在評估中，但仍積極追蹤該個股。 
限制評等：為遵循相關法令規章及/或元大之政策，暫不給予評等及目標價。

註：元大給予個股之目標價係依 12個月投資期間計算。大中華探索系列報告並無正式之 12個月目標價，其投資建議乃根據分析師報告中之指定期間分析
而得。

## Document 623: 凱基投顧_2379 瑞昱_劉宇程_20261029

Source PDF: all report pdf/凱基投顧_2379 瑞昱_劉宇程_20261029.pdf
Report source: line報告備份
Date: 2026-10-29
Pages: 8
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

十月 29, 2026 研究網站: https://investment.kgisia.com.tw/Portal/Report/Index/Zh/R  Powered by the EFA Platform 1

動態更新  IC設計 ‧ 台灣

瑞昱 
(2379.TW /2379 TT)

規格升級與價格調整抵銷 PC/NB 需求疲軟 
重要訊息 
瑞昱召開 3Q26 法說會。  
評論及分析  
3Q26 財報符合預期。 瑞昱 3Q26 營收達 377 億元，季增 0.5%、年增
27.9%，主因客戶需求保持 穩健，其中又 以網通產品為主要成長動能。儘管
PC 需求疲弱，營收仍維持高檔，係受惠： (1)高規格產品占比提升帶動平均
單價提升；及(2)價格調整部分轉嫁成本上升。上述因素亦支撐 3Q26 毛利率
達 47.4%，季增 0.4 個百分點，年減 2.2 個百分點。在業外收益 6.06 億元
挹注下，使 3Q26 EPS 達 8.05 元，符合我們預估。  
4Q26 PC 需求續弱，但產品組合與價格調整可望抵銷季節性衰退。 管理層表
示 4Q26 動能保持穩健，在產品組合改善與價格調整推升 ASP 下，營收不致
於出現往常的季節性下滑。不過 PC 需求預期將進一步走弱，主因： (1)客戶
在 1H26 提前拉貨；(2) DRAM 與 CPU 成本上漲壓抑終端需求。 通路庫存大
致健康，未見刻意囤貨跡象。如同我們在 十月 Radar monthly 中所述，具備
規格升級、產品組合改善與訂價能力的台灣 IC 設計公司，營收及毛利率相
對較能獲得支撐。毛利率方面，管理層重申 2H26 毛利率將逐步回升，我們
亦認為漲價效益逐步反映亦 可提供支撐。我們上修 4Q26 營收預估至 378 億
元，季增 0.2%、年增 43.8%；毛利率預估上修至 47.9%，季增 0.5 個百分
點、年減 0.2 個百分點。  
2027 年規格升級支撐 營收成長，成本壓力則限縮毛利率改善空間。 管理層
預期 2027 年營收將穩健成長，係受惠 ：(1)產品組合優化與價格調整推升整
體 ASP；(2)規格升級，包括 Wi-Fi 7 滲透率由 2026 年約 30%提升至約
40%、multi -gig 乙太網路與可管理型交換器採用率擴大，以及 Wi-Fi 8 於
2H27 開始量產爬坡； (3)車用乙太網路與 100G 光通訊 PHY 等新產品，惟
PC 仍為主要不確定性因素，目前尚未看見全面復甦的跡象。成本方面，晶
圓代工與封測成本仍具 不確定性、 AI 伺服器持續排擠產能，供給預期要到
2027 年才會明顯改善。管理層 表示成本上升幅度已超出公司可合理吸收的
範圍，因此自 6 月起即逐案與客戶討論價格調整，但目標為維 持毛利率穩
定，不會單純為拉高毛利率而漲價。綜合考量：(1)晶圓代工與封測成本壓力
將持續； (2)價格調整僅能部分轉嫁； (3)產品組合與規格升級的效益，我們
上修 2027 年營收預估 至 1,602 億元，年增 7.2%，下修毛利 率預估至
48.4%，年增 0.4 個百分點。 
投資建議  
基於： (1)營收預估上修，反映漲價與規格升級； (2)毛利率預估下修 ，反映
晶圓代工及封測成本壓力僅部分轉嫁 ，整體而言我們 分別上修 2026-27 年
EPS 預估至 32.50 元(年增 13.0%)，及 37.86 元(年增 16.5%)。目標價小幅
上調至 680 元，基於 2027 年 EPS 預估 18 倍不變。維持「持有」評等。  
投資風險  
終端需求不振；供應鏈成本上揚對 ASP、利潤率形成壓力；競爭加劇。

ESG 圖表定義  
項目 定義 說明 
能源使用 
直接與間接能源消耗總量(十億焦耳) 
- 公司運營範圍內消耗之能源總量 
- 能源使用總量 = 直接能源消耗總量 + 間接能源消耗量 
- 總能源使用量包括購買的能源、生產的能源 
- 就公用事業而言，輸電/電網損耗為其業務活動之一部分，被視為總能源消耗，數據不包括
為滿足能源使用而生產的電力(公用事業為出售而生產) 
- 就公用事業而言，用於能源生產的煤、天然氣或核能等原料不屬於「總能源使用」項下

購買之再生能源 
Primary再生能源購買總量(十億焦耳) 
- 公司各種來源之能源消耗量與購買的能源中屬於自然界可再生者(太陽能、風能、水能、生
物質能、地熱能)之量 
- 如無證據顯示再生能源由公司生產，則所報告的能源數據視為購買的再生能源

再生能源使用率 再生能源占總能源消耗量之比例  
CO2 約當排放量 
直接 CO2與 CO2約當排放量(公噸) 
- 公司擁有或控制的來源的直接排放量(範圍 1排放量) 
- 相關氣體：二氧化碳(CO2)、甲烷(CH4)、一氧化二氮(N2O)、氫氟碳化物(HFCS)、全氟化合
物(PFCS)、六氟化硫(SF6)、三氟化氮(NF3)

CO2 約當排放量營收比 
直接 CO2與 CO2約當排放量(公噸)銷售(百萬元)比 
- 公司擁有或控制的來源的直接排放量(範圍 1排放量) 
- 相關氣體：二氧化碳(CO2)、甲烷(CH4)、一氧化二氮(N2O)、氫氟碳化物(HFCS)、全氟化合
物(PFCS)、六氟化硫(SF6)、三氟化氮(NF3)

廢棄物總量 
廢棄物總量(公噸) 
- 總廢棄物 = 無害廢棄物 + 有害廢棄物 
- 僅考慮固體廢棄物，但如液體廢棄物以公噸為單位呈報，則會將其加入求得包含液體廢棄
物之總量 
- 對於採礦、石油與天然氣等行業，tailings、廢石、煤、飛灰等廢棄物亦考慮在內

廢棄物回收率 
公司呈報的廢棄物回收率 
- 廢棄物回收率 = 廢棄物回收量/總廢棄物*100 
- 廢棄物轉化為能源或經由廢棄物焚燒產生能源視為廢棄物回收 
- 經由堆肥回收的廢棄物視為回收之廢棄物

總取水量 
總取水量(立方公尺) 
- 由呈報組織直接或經水公司等中介機構從任何水源抽取的總水量 
- 水井、城鎮/公用事業/市政用水、河水、地表水等不同之水源均予以考慮

環保支出 
環保支出總金額 
- 所有用於環境保護，或防止、減少、控制環境因素、影響、危害的投資與支出，亦包括處
置、處理、衛生、清理支出

員工流動率 
員工流動率 
- 包括任何原因(自願或非自願)離開公司的員工，如辭職、退休、自然離職/死亡、醫療失
能、冗員、裁員、重組、解僱、裁減或定期合約到期 
- 員工流動率 = (離開之員工/平均員工人數)*100 
- 平均員工人數 = (本年末員工人數 + 去年末員工人數)/2 
- 本財年末員工數 = 上個財年末員工數 + 新員工數 - 離開之員工數

女性經理 
女性經理百分比 
- 女性經理占公司經理的百分比 
- 如有不同階層之百分比，如最高階、高階、中階、初階，則會考慮中階女性經理之百分比 
- 女性經理百分比 = 女性經理人數/經理總數*100

女性員工 
女性員工百分比 
- 女性員工占公司員工總數的百分比 
- 女性員工百分比 = 女性人數/員工總數*100

教育訓練總時數 
所有員工教育訓練總時數 
- 僅考慮員工教育訓練時數 
- 包括一般員工所有類型的教育訓練(如健康與安全、環境、急難事件救援、技能與職業發展) 
- 如果數據以天為單位，則乘以 8，係假設 1天 = 8小時工作

每位員工教育訓練時數 平均每年每位員工總教育訓練時數  
資料來源： Refinitiv ，凱基

### Page 1

十月 28, 2026 研究網站: https://investment.kgisia.com.tw/Portal/Report/Index/Zh/R  Powered by the EFA Platform 1

動態更新  記憶體 ‧ 台灣

宜鼎 
(5289.TWO /5289 TT )

持續深化 AI 基礎建設布局 
重要訊息 
我們針對宜鼎 3Q26 財報進行前瞻，並分析後續營運展望。 
評論及分析  
預估 3Q26 毛利率將小幅下滑。3Q26 營收 258.8 億元，季增 15.3%。我們
推估營收成長主要反映： (1)DRAM/NAND 位元出貨量大致持平；
(2)DRAM/NAND ASP 季增 10-20%；(3)安提貢獻營收 4-5 億元。受記憶體報
價漲幅收斂影響，預估毛利率季減 4.5ppts 至 60.5%，每股盈餘 117.1 元。 
AI 基礎建設比重提升 ，客戶結構 持續優化。宜鼎客戶結構近一至兩年顯著
轉變，由工控與 IPC 逐步轉向 AI 基礎建設。我們預估 後者營收比重已超過
40%，主要客戶涵蓋 Switch 大廠、GPU 大廠及美系/中系 CSP，供應產品包
括 DDR4 顆粒、DDR5 RDIMM 及企業級儲存產品( 以 TLC 為主)。展望 2027
年，我們預期上述應用營收將持續穩健成長，其中企業級儲存有望加速放
量。公司過去以 SLC/MLC 及 BiCS3/ 5 小容量 TLC 為主，今年起逐步轉向
BiCS8，多數客戶預計於 2027 年完成驗證並放量出貨，主要應用於 boot 
drive 與 eSSD。隨產品升級及客戶驗證陸續完成，預估 2027 年 AI 基礎建設
營收比重有望超過 50%，趨近整體產業平均 ，客戶結構持續優化 。  
2027 年營收穩健成長，報價漲幅收斂使獲利 微幅下滑 。我們預估安提
2026- 27 年營收分別達 21.9 億/30.4 億元，年增 169.4% /38.5%，主要動能
來自： (1)記憶體漲價帶動整機 ASP 提升； (2)兩大人型機器人客戶出貨成
長。惟記憶體本業規模快速擴大，安提營收占比已降至約 2%，後續占比提
升將取決於人型機器人能否大規模放量，以及 AI ASIC 客戶能否順利量產。
展望 2027 年，隨 AI 基礎建設營收比重提升及台系廠商加入供應鏈，宜鼎可
望維持穩定貨源。我們預估 DRAM /NAND 位元出貨量分別年增 4%/35%，
其中 NAND 主要受惠 TLC 比重提升；DRAM /NAND ASP 則分別年增
21%/18%，帶動全年營收年增 38.5%至 1,234.2 億元。惟報價漲幅趨緩，
預估毛利率回落至 43.3%，EPS 為 375.1 元，年減 5.4%。 
投資建議  
考量目前股價僅交易於 2026 年 2.7 倍本益比，評價具吸引力，凱基維持對
宜鼎正向看法，重申「增加持股」評等，目標價 1,500 元，隱含 2027 年 4
倍本益比，低於模組廠龍頭群聯的 7 倍。 
投資風險  
記憶體供貨短缺、毛利率降幅超出預期 。 
增加持股‧維持 
  收盤價 October 28 (NT$)   1,160

優化。

斂使獲利微幅下滑 。

### Page 3

台灣 宜鼎

十月 28, 2026 研究網站: https://investment.kgisia.com.tw/Portal/Report/Index/Zh/R 3

圖 3: 公司概況  圖 4: 記憶體模組佔宜鼎營收大宗 
宜鼎成立於 2005 年，並於 2013 年正式掛牌上櫃，為一家以
服務為導向的工業用儲存裝置供應商，生產基地位於新北汐止
及宜蘭。公司專注於企業級、工業、航太、 醫療與國防等應用
市場，提供工業用嵌入式儲存裝置與工業用動態隨機記憶體模
組與相關技術服務，並以自有品牌「 Innodisk 」行銷全球。宜
鼎結合集團旗下公司 安提、維新、安捷科、巽晨，產品涵蓋記
憶體模組到 AIoT 周邊產品，提供客戶從雲端到邊緣端，一站
式完整 AI 解決方案。

## Document 711: 宏观：国内PMI回落，海外紧缩升温

Source PDF: all report pdf/宏观：国内PMI回落，海外紧缩升温.pdf
Report source: 浙商期货
Date: 2026-11-02
Pages: 38
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

宏 观 ： 国 内 P M I 回 落 ， 海 外 紧 缩 升 温宏 观 ： 国 内 P M I 回 落 ， 海 外 紧 缩 升 温
期货研究报告
发布时间：2026.10.31
适用周期：2026.10.31~2026.11.7
浙商期货有限公司期货交易咨询业务资格：证监许可[2011]1287号
【撰写人】浙商期货有限公司 郑弘（Z0015227）
【咨询电话】0571-87215357
一、观点综述
核心结论：美联储、欧洲央行与日本央行10月议息会议集体按兵不动但整体偏鹰，12月再度加息预期升温；10月30日美股科技巨头财报不及预期引发
大跌，拖累全球风险资产，国际金价跌破4000美元/盎司创近一年新低，国际油价因霍尔木兹海峡油轮遇袭大涨；国内10月制造业PMI回落至49.3%重
回收缩区间，经济景气阶段性走弱，A股震荡下行、上证指数失守3600点。
逻辑链条：
① 海外央行偏鹰：美联储10比2维持利率不变，欧央行维持2.50%，日本央行8比1维持1.25%，12月加息概率约七成。

② 海外市场：科技财报暴雷引发纳指单日-2.86%，金价跌破4000美元，WTI涨至104.12美元。
③ 国内数据：10月制造业PMI 49.3%、较上月-0.8个百分点，非制造业49.7%同样回落，上证指数-2.48%。
风险提示：12月海外央行集体加息预期与全球利率高位；科技股调整与AI资本开支预期回落引发流动性踩踏；中东局势与油价大涨推升输入性通胀；国内经济景气阶段性回落与A股风险偏
好走弱。
上周资产变化
数据说明：最新为10月30日收盘或时点值；A股与商品为10月30日收盘，美股指数为10月30日美股收盘，变化为最新值较窗口首个交易日10月23日；收益率数值上升标红、下降标绿，USDCNY数
值下降代表人民币升值并标绿。

二、国内经济情况
注：9月工业增加值、社会消费品零售总额、固定资产投资与三季度GDP已于10月19日公布，9月物价与金融数据于10月14日至15日公布，9月进出口于10月13日公布；10月采购经理指数于10月
31日公布，为窗口内唯一新公布数据。
1. 国内数据总览

### Page 3

总量数据：三季度GDP同比+4.5%、环比+1.2%，前三季度累计+4.6%，落在全年目标区间内；GDP平减指数连续两个季度为正。
2. 国内社消零售
9月社零当月同比+1.1%、较8月回升0.7个百分点，主要受中秋假期前置于9月的节日消费带动，粮油食品类+6.7%、烟酒类与餐饮收入同步抬升；通讯器材类+31.1%
受益于数码新品集中上市，汽车类-21.2%降幅继续扩大。1-9月服务零售额同比+5.0%，继续强于商品消费。

### Page 6

3. 固定资产投资
1-9月固投累计同比-7.2%、降幅与前值持平，9月环比转正增长0.07%；制造业投资累计-2.1%、降幅收窄0.2个百分点，为4月以来首次收窄，基建-4.2%小幅走弱，房
地产-20.6%降幅扩大0.7个百分点。地产端新开工-25.0%、竣工-25.1%，投资端压力仍在累积。

### Page 9

4. 进出口情况
9月以美元计价出口+36.3%、进口+31.0%，前三季度进出口总额同比增长18.8%；出口分项同步更新至9月，自动数据处理设备+93.3%、高新技术产品+63.9%、汽
车+61.9%，AI相关电子链与外需韧性共同构成主要拉动。

### Page 12

5. 工业增加值
9月规模以上工业增加值当月同比+5.5%、较8月加快0.3个百分点，增速连续两个月抬升；制造业增长6.0%基本持平，采矿业由负转正至1.3%，公用事业加快至5.6%。
新动能产品产量维持较快增长，工业机器人+18.9%、新能源汽车+16.9%、集成电路+18.4%，均为当月产量同比口径。

### Page 15

6. 国内价格指标
9月CPI当月同比+0.9%、与8月持平，核心CPI+1.0%；PPI当月同比+5.0%、较8月回升1.2个百分点并创年内新高，环比上涨1.2%，前三季度累计同比+2.3%。价格修
复延续，通胀环境较上半年明显好转。

### Page 18

7. 金融数据
9月社融存量同比+7.1%、较8月回落0.1个百分点，M2同比+7.2%、回落0.3个百分点，M1同比+3.2%、回落0.9个百分点，M2与M1剪刀差走阔至4.0个百分点。10月
贷款市场报价利率维持不变，1年期3.0%、5年期以上3.5%，为连续第17个月持平；央行10月MLF净回笼2000亿元。

### Page 21

8. 景气指数
10月官方制造业PMI 49.3%、较9月回落0.8个百分点并重回收缩区间，受十一假期前部分需求提前释放以及国际环境复杂多变等因素影响，供需两端均有放缓，生产指
数与新订单指数分别为49.8%与49.4%；高技术制造业与装备制造业PMI分别为51.3%与50.6%，继续高于临界点，新动能仍保持较快增长。非制造业商务活动指数
49.7%、回落0.5个百分点，服务业商务活动指数50.0%持平荣枯线。财新制造业PMI仍为9月的52.1%。

### Page 22

三、海外经济情况

### Page 23

注：美联储10月27日至28日议息会议决议于北京时间10月29日凌晨公布，欧洲央行于10月29日公布利率决议并维持不变，日本央行于10月30日公布利率决议并维持不变；美国9月PCE物价指数与
三季度GDP初值于10月29日公布。

宫格说明：10月27日至28日议息会议已决定维持利率不变，CME FedWatch 对应的会议基准自10月28日起由10月会议切换至12月会议，上期10月29日79.6%与本期72.4%同为12月会议口径、可

直接比较；期内12月加息定价最高触及85.1%。美元兑日元较上期小幅回落，日元略有升值。
2. 美国通胀情况
美国9月PCE物价指数于10月29日公布，同比+3.6%、较8月回升0.2个百分点并创今年5月以来新高，环比+0.5%；核心PCE同比+3.1%、较8月回升0.1个百分点，环比
+0.3%，两项均符合预期。能源价格反弹是整体通胀回升的主要推手，但剔除能源后核心通胀同样未能延续下行，非周期性通胀高于周期性通胀，通胀粘性有所增强。

### Page 26

3. 美国就业情况
9月新增非农仅+2.9万人、远低于预期的9万人，7月与8月分别下修至-1.0万人与13.3万人；失业率升至4.2%，平均时薪同比回落至3.0%并为2021年以来最慢，实际时
薪已连续第六个月下降。就业偏弱与通胀偏强并存，是美联储10月选择观望的重要依据。

### Page 29

4. 美国经济情况
美国三季度GDP环比折年率初值+3.4%、高于预期的3.2%与前值2.2%，增长韧性强于预期；9月零售销售环比+0.8%，但主要由汽油涨价拉动，剔除汽油与汽车后的核
心零售增速偏弱；9月ISM制造业PMI 54.5%、非制造业PMI 54.9%，均处扩张区间。个人收入环比+0.2%、消费支出环比+0.5%，双双不及预期，个人储蓄率降至
2022年10月以来最低。本周谷歌、英特尔、亚马逊等科技巨头财报不及预期，10月30日纳斯达克指数单日下跌2.86%、为6月以来最大单日跌幅，道指与标普分别下跌
1.09%与1.82%。

### Page 32

5. 美联储预期
美联储10月27日至28日议息会议以10比2维持联邦基金利率于3.75%至4.00%不变，理事库克与克利夫兰联储主席哈马克主张加息25bp、投下反对票，声明将经济评估
由稳健上调至强劲，重申通胀仍处高位；沃什在发布会上拒绝给出前瞻指引，并宣布2027年至2028年逐步减少议息会议的利率表决频次，弱化对市场预期的引导。欧
洲央行与日本央行同期均按兵不动，但整体信号偏鹰：欧洲央行明确表示可能在12月加息并警告能源价格推升通胀风险，日本央行首次明确基础通胀已达到2%目标、
市场预计12月再次加息。议息落地后12月加息概率一度升至85.1%，截至10月30日为72.4%；美债10年期收益率降至4.94%、2年期降至4.56%。

### Page 35

6. 日本经济情况
日本9月CPI同比+2.1%、核心CPI+2.1%，较8月的1.9%与1.7%双双回升，通胀重新接近2%目标；日本央行10月30日以8比1维持政策利率于1.25%不变，为1995年以
来最高水平，委员高田创主张加息至1.5%、投下反对票，央行首次明确基础通胀已达到2%目标并宣布从明年1月起加快ETF持仓出售速度。市场普遍预期日本央行12月
再次加息。美元兑日元本周小幅回落至157.58，日元略有升值。

### Page 37
